--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I'll analyze the foraging mission carefully.

**Mission Analysis:**
- Score decreases by 1 for delivering the "good" item (one color half), increases by 1 for delivering the "bad" item
- Need to: go to the correct source half (blue or green), pick up items, return to nest (white floor)
- Key perceptions: floor color (black=source, white=nest, gray=middle), wall colors (red=nest side, blue/green=source halves)

**Strategy:**
- We need robots to go toward one specific color (say BLUE=color 3) source half, then return to nest (go toward RED=color 1)
- But we don't know which color is "good" initially - need to explore both
- Core loop: Explore → detect source (black floor) → return to nest (go toward red wall)
- Better: Go toward blue source, pick up, go toward red wall (nest)

**State design:**
- State 0: Explore/GoToColor(blue) - searching for blue source
- State 1: GoToColor(red) - return to nest with item
- Transition: black floor → go to nest; white floor → back to exploring

Let me design 14 machines exploring different topologies and parameters.

**Color codes:** Red=1, Green=2, Blue=3, Yellow=4, Magenta=5, Cyan=6

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I need to see the results from Round 2 to know which machines performed best. Since no scores were provided, I'll reason from first principles and diversify further.

**Key insight:** We don't know which color (blue=3 or green=2) gives the "good" item. The robots must go to the source, pick up items, and return to nest. The nest has a RED wall (color=1) and WHITE floor. The source has BLACK floor. We need to minimize score, so we want robots delivering the item that DECREASES score by 1.

**Refined strategy:**
- The critical unknown is which source half is "good". Try both blue and green exclusively.
- Core loop: GoToColor(target) → on black floor → GoToColor(red/nest) → on white floor → repeat
- Also try: Exploration → black floor → GoToColor(red) → white floor → back
- The "return to nest" phase should use GoToColor(red=1) since the nest wall is RED
- Add robustness: if on white floor (delivered), go back to searching

I'll keep the best-looking topologies from round 1 (simple 2-state with clear trigger) and add variations with tighter probabilities and different combinations.

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 8 --vel0 1.0 --clr0 3 --cle0 4 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 0 --p0x0 0.95 --s1 8 --vel1 1.0 --clr1 1 --cle1 5 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 2 --p1x0 0.95

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
Since I haven't received explicit scores, I need to reason carefully about what should work best and diversify my approach.

**Key insights I may be missing:**
1. The robots start in the CENTER of the arena, not at the nest. So GoToColor(blue/green) from center might work.
2. The nest wall is RED (color=1). The source walls are BLUE (color=3) or GREEN (color=2).
3. "Entering a half gives the robot an item" - so just being on black floor gives an item automatically.
4. The critical question: which color source decreases score? We must try BOTH and let evolution select.
5. When returning to nest, robots should see RED wall and move toward it.
6. A robot already carrying an item that enters the OTHER source half gets the wrong item - so we should avoid the wrong half.

**Better topology:** 3 states:
- State 0: GoToColor(blue OR green) - seek source
- State 1: GoToColor(red) - return to nest (triggered by black floor)
- State 2: GoToColor(source) - triggered by white floor (delivered, go back)

The simplest robust design: GoToColor(target_source) → black floor → GoToColor(red) → white floor → repeat.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
Since I still haven't received explicit scores, I need to think more carefully about what might be going wrong with my designs.

**Critical re-examination:**

1. The score is MINIMISED. Delivering one item type gives -1 (good), the other +1 (bad). We want negative scores.
2. The robots need to consistently visit ONE source half and return to nest repeatedly.
3. My designs may be failing because: robots oscillate between source halves, or the "return to nest" trigger is unreliable.

**Key problem with my current designs:**
- Using "white floor → go back to source" might cause robots to immediately turn around after just touching white floor briefly.
- The transition probabilities may be too high, causing premature state switches.
- I should try LOWER probabilities for floor-based transitions to require more sustained contact.

**Better approach:**
- Use probability ~0.3-0.5 for floor transitions (fires each step, so sustained contact gives ~(1-p)^n chance of NOT switching)
- At p=0.5, after 5 steps on black floor: 97% chance of having switched. At p=0.1, after 5 steps: 41% chance. Lower p = more time needed on floor.
- Actually p=0.95 means almost instant switching which should be fine.

**New idea:** Maybe I should try GoAwayColor(red) to push robots TOWARD source (away from red wall = toward black), and GoToColor(red) to return.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I notice I've been designing blind without feedback scores. Let me think more carefully about what's fundamentally right or wrong.

**Deep re-analysis:**

The mission says "entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying." So a robot gets an item just by being on black floor in that half.

**Critical issue I've been ignoring:** The source is divided into two halves - one blue-walled, one green-walled. A robot navigating GoToColor(blue) will approach the BLUE WALL, which means it arrives at the blue half. Same for green. This is correct.

**But:** When returning, GoToColor(red) navigates toward the RED NEST WALL. This should work.

**What might be failing:** The 2-state machine with p=0.95 on floor transition should work well. Maybe the issue is that robots are getting stuck or the wrong color is "good". I should hedge by trying BOTH colors equally and also trying completely different approaches.

**New idea to try:** Use GoAwayColor(green) to steer toward blue side, and vice versa. Also try attraction/repulsion-based homing.

**Most important hedge:** Submit machines targeting BOTH blue AND green with identical structure, so evolution can select the right one.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I've been designing blind for 6 rounds without score feedback. Let me fundamentally reconsider.

**Core problem reassessment:**

The score is minimised. One source half gives -1 per delivery, the other +1. Without knowing which is which, I need BOTH blue-targeting and green-targeting machines. Evolution should select the correct one.

**What the simplest correct machine looks like:**
- State 0: GoToColor(blue/green) → when on black floor → switch to State 1
- State 1: GoToColor(red/nest) → when on white floor → switch back to State 0

This is exactly what my 2-state machines do. They SHOULD work. The fact that I haven't seen good results suggests either:
1. The machines are syntactically broken somehow
2. The probability condition fires too rarely/often
3. Something else is fundamentally wrong

**Let me check my FSM syntax carefully:** The floor condition (C=0 for black) fires with probability p each step when on black floor. At p=0.95, after 1 step on black floor there's 95% chance of switching. This seems right.

**New insight:** Maybe I should try p=1.0 for floor transitions (deterministic) and also try very different structures. Also, I notice in FSM 13 of round 5, I accidentally wrote `--p3x0` twice - that was a bug. Let me be more careful.

**Also trying:** GoToColor(yellow=4, magenta=5, cyan=6) - wait, those are LED colors not wall colors. Wall colors available to perceive: red=1, green=2, blue=3, yellow=4, magenta=5, cyan=6. The arena has RED, BLUE, GREEN walls. So colors 1, 2, 3 are the relevant ones.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I've been running 7 rounds without score feedback, which means I need to be even more systematic. Let me think about what could fundamentally be wrong.

**Critical realization:** I've been assuming GoToColor works by moving toward colored WALLS. But the color perception might be of OTHER ROBOTS' LEDs, not walls. Let me re-read: "GoToColor → moves toward the nearest object of a target colour" and condition C=7 "the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera." The arena has colored wall BLOCKS. So robots CAN perceive wall colors.

**But wait:** If robots are navigating GoToColor(blue=3) and blue walls are behind the blue source half, robots will approach those walls - which IS the blue source. This should work. GoToColor(red=1) toward the red nest wall should bring them back.

**Maybe the problem is simpler:** Perhaps I've been making parameter errors. Let me try the absolute simplest possible machines with clean syntax and verify each one carefully.

**Also new idea:** What if I use LED colors to create a trail? Robots carrying items (in state "return") emit a color, other robots can perceive them and follow/avoid. But this adds complexity.

**Most likely issue:** The machines ARE working but scores are near 0 (random delivery) because I don't know which color is good. Let me commit heavily to BOTH colors with very clean, simple designs.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
After 8 rounds without score feedback, I need to radically reconsider my approach.

**Fundamental rethink:**

My designs have been consistently using the same pattern. If they were working well, I'd expect to see scores. The lack of feedback suggests either all scores are similar (near 0) or I'm missing something basic.

**What if the problem is:** GoToColor navigates toward the NEAREST object of that color. If robots have LEDs of those colors, they might be following each other instead of walls! The wall blocks are the colored objects, but if no robot has that LED color, GoToColor should find walls.

**Critical insight I've missed:** Maybe the transition triggers are wrong. When a robot is on BLACK floor and switches to "GoToColor(red)", it still needs to LEAVE the black floor first. If it's deep in the source area and GoToColor(red) pulls it back, that's fine. But if the probability is 1.0, the robot switches immediately on first contact with black - which might be correct.

**New hypothesis:** Maybe robots are getting stuck at walls. GoToColor(blue) → robot hits blue wall → stuck. Need exploration component or repulsion from walls.

**Another idea:** Use Exploration in searching phase (random walk finds source by chance), then GoToColor(red) to return. Simpler and avoids wall-hugging.

**Also:** Try GoAwayColor(green) to push toward blue side (since green is opposite side).

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
This is the final round. I need to make my best possible submission.

**Final analysis after 9 rounds of blind design:**

The core foraging loop should be: seek source → get item → return to nest → deliver → repeat.

The fundamental design that MUST work logically:
- GoToColor(blue or green) finds the colored source wall → robot enters black floor → picks up item automatically
- GoToColor(red) finds the red nest wall → robot enters white floor → delivers item
- Floor color triggers the state switch

The cleanest possible FSM:
- State 0: GoToColor(target_source), transition on BLACK floor (p=1.0) → State 1
- State 1: GoToColor(red=1), transition on WHITE floor (p=1.0) → State 0

Since I don't know which source color is "good", I submit BOTH. Evolution selects the better one.

**For the final round, I'll maximize coverage:** 6 machines targeting blue, 6 targeting green, with slight variations in structure to ensure robustness. I'll also include exploration-based variants that avoid wall-hugging issues.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
